13 common sourdough mistakes and what each one really costs a loaf

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Home bakers make the same 13 sourdough slips. See which ones ruin a loaf, which only blemish it, and which signs to trust over the timer.

A home baker can follow a sourdough recipe to the letter, measuring every gram and setting every timer, and still pull a different loaf from the oven each time. The reason is that a starter is a living culture, and no two starters are quite alike. Smithsonian reports that in a study led by microbiologist Anne Madden of North Carolina State University and her colleagues, starters made with identical flour each carried a different mix of microbes. Two people with the same instructions are therefore not working with the same ingredient. Because the mix of microbes varies from jar to jar, the hours in any recipe are a rough guide rather than a promise. Most of the mistakes that follow have the same cause: the baker trusts the calendar or the clock instead of watching what the starter and the dough are actually doing.

These mistakes do not all cost the same thing, and the costliest can be hard to see. A loaf that spreads flat or a crumb that stays gummy shows the problem right away. Lost flavor is quieter. The tang of sourdough comes from lactic acid and acetic acid, according to Smithsonian. A baker can't add that taste at the end, and a loaf rushed through its stages can rise well, look right and still taste like ordinary bread. Some warnings that bakers repeat are milder than they sound, and getting them wrong costs little that anyone would notice at the table. Knowing which mistakes ruin a loaf and which ones only blemish it saves a home baker time, flour and frustration.

Allrecipes set out to identify the errors home bakers commonly make when baking sourdough. These are familiar slips from everyday kitchens, so the collection works as a practical checklist for anyone with a jar of starter on the counter. The mistakes range widely. One concerns what comes out of the tap, and another concerns how a baker slashes the loaf before it goes into the oven. A third concerns the starter most people pour down the drain. Each mistake raises two questions: which sign a baker should trust in place of the timer, and how much getting it wrong really costs. The slides that follow answer both questions for every item.

Bakers who have just stirred flour and water into a jar want bread from it right away, and giving in to that urge ranks first among common sourdough mistakes, according to Allrecipes. A fresh starter needs roughly a week of maturing before it can raise a loaf. The error is about timing rather than technique, and a baker makes it before any dough is mixed. That week is a minimum, and a starter is not guaranteed to be ready when it ends. Bakers who adopt a piece of an established starter can skip the wait, because the warning applies only to a culture grown from scratch.

Even a full week may be too early. Smithsonian reports that a starter doesn't settle into a steady state, with yeasts producing enough gas to lift dough, until somewhere between Days 10 and 14. By that later timeline, a loaf baked after a few days, or even after a week, could come from a starter that hasn't yet settled. The practical course is to start checking around the one-week mark and to expect that a second week may still be needed. While they wait, bakers can influence which microbes take hold through their choice of flour, how warm they keep the room, and their feeding schedule.

The common trap is reading activity in the jar as readiness. "Yes, your starter might be bubbly, but that doesn't make it strong," Allrecipes author Stacey Ballis said. Bubbles show that the culture is alive, but a starter is ready only when its yeasts make enough gas to raise a loaf.

Readiness depends on what the starter can do, not on how many days have passed. A baker who waits for the starter to show that strength loses a few days and avoids baking a flat loaf.

Home bakers have reason to worry about the most ordinary ingredient they use. Allrecipes ranks unfiltered tap water second among 13 common sourdough mistakes. Most people never think twice about the water they pour into a starter, so it is unsettling to hear that water from the faucet could put the jar at risk. The concern is that chlorine in untreated tap water can kill the yeast a starter depends on. The open question is how often that happens in a home kitchen.

The danger may be smaller than its ranking suggests. "Some recipes advise using bottled or filtered water because chlorine can kill yeast, but I've never had a problem with chlorinated Toronto tap water," Jessica Brooks, a digital producer and pro-trained cook and baker at CBC Life, said. Neither side has settled the matter. A cautious baker can filter the water to guard against a possible problem. Still, tap water is no sure way to lose a loaf, and nobody needs to throw out a starter because it was fed from the faucet.

The bigger risk is a wrong diagnosis, because a baker who finds a sluggish jar may blame the tap and change the wrong thing. CBC reports that a new starter normally goes through a slowdown in activity. A quiet jar during that early slowdown may have nothing to do with the water. The real test is the culture itself. If a starter bubbles and rises on tap water, the water is fine.

Switch to filtered water only if the starter stays flat after that normal early slowdown, and treat the switch as a cheap experiment rather than a rescue.

Water looks like the one ingredient in sourdough that needs no thought, a neutral liquid poured between the flour and the starter. Bakers can still get its temperature wrong at every stage, from feeding the starter to mixing the dough, according to Allrecipes. A baker who pours carelessly can harm the culture well before any loaf exists. The temperature of that water sets how fast the culture works. Yeast works more slowly in cold water and can die in water that is too hot, and Julie Van Rosendaal, who writes recipes for CBC News, says a starter grows faster when it is warm and slower when it is cold. A baker who misses the mark needs to know less about one perfect number than about which side of it the water landed on.

A starter recovers from cold water, even though the warning sounds severe. Stacey Ballis, an author and recipe developer, warned that water that is too cold will "shock the yeast." Bakers also chill starters on purpose to slow them down. Van Rosendaal keeps her starter in the refrigerator and feeds it once a week, according to CBC News, and she revives a dormant starter by feeding it at room temperature for one to two days. A chilled culture loses days, not its life. The price of too-cold water is a slower start, and the remedy is to wait for the starter to warm up and grow active again rather than judge it by the clock.

Too-hot water is the riskier error, because it can kill the yeast rather than only slow it. Heat harms yeast in other settings too, and drying a starter in even a low oven kills the yeast, according to CBC News. When unsure, pour the water cooler rather than warmer, and judge the result by how lively the starter becomes rather than by how warm the water felt.

A cooler pour costs a baker some time, and a hotter one can cost the yeast.

Rushing a sourdough loaf throws away the reason to bake one. When you want bread by dinner, the temptation is to squeeze every stage, but Allrecipes reports that sourdough gets its texture from a slow, cold fermentation, and a hurried loaf loses what sets it apart. Commercial yeast is the tool for same-day bread. The Santa Fe New Mexican reports that Andree Falls, owner of Sage Bakehouse in Santa Fe, N.M., considers yeast-raised dough too quick to become anything distinctive. A rushed sourdough therefore ends up as a weaker version of a yeast loaf. Impatience is less a technical slip than the wrong choice of bread for the time you have.

Waiting is not a luxury reserved for hobbyists with empty weekends. Sage Bakehouse uses no commercial yeast at all and raises its bread only with sourdough starter, according to The Santa Fe New Mexican. The bakery keeps that slow pace while turning out 700 to 1,000 loaves a day. A home baker with a single loaf has even less reason to cut the wait short.

Patience means judging the dough, not setting the timer longer and walking away. Serious bakers plan in hours and even days, and no single number is the right one. Stacey Ballis, who wrote the Allrecipes guide, has rested her dough 24 to 72 hours in the fridge before shaping. Sage Bakehouse averages 8 to 10 hours of fermentation after mixing, according to The Santa Fe New Mexican. The first is one baker's practice, and the second is one bakery's average. Even with an average to go by, the bakery lets the dough decide when it is ready. "We say the bread is the boss," Falls said. For a home baker, the practical change is to plan the day around the dough rather than the dough around the day.

The urge to hurry comes back during the autolyse, gluten development and proofing. The Santa Fe New Mexican reports that Falls sees good baking as dedicated care for the dough from the start of its process to the end.

A baker who learns to wait at this stage avoids several of the mistakes still ahead.

The autolyse is easy to cut because nothing seems to happen during it. Flour, water and starter sit in a bowl while the baker waits. That rest does real work, though. Resting the mix without salt hydrates the flour and improves the loaf's rise, and the step adds only about 45 minutes to the bake, according to Allrecipes. A baker who skips it moves into the rest of the bake with flour that hasn't had that time to hydrate. The step depends on holding back the salt as much as on waiting, so skipping the autolyse means adding an ingredient early, not just saving time. That makes the shortcut a poor bargain. The baker saves less than an hour and gives up some of the rise, one of the results anyone can see when the loaf comes out of the oven.

The advice comes with no exception attached. "I recommend you do not skip this step. Period," said Stacey Ballis, who wrote the Allrecipes guide. The fix asks for no special skill, tool or difficult judgment. It asks only for a pause, with the salt kept out of the bowl until the pause is over. The length of that pause is approximate, too. The goal is to give the flour time with its water before the salt goes in, not to hit an exact mark on the clock. A baker who reads the dough this way can set the timer loosely.

Of all the steps a hurried baker might cut, this one costs the least to keep.

A baker checks gluten with the hands, not with a timer. Dough without enough gluten fails the windowpane test, in which a baker stretches a small piece between the fingers, and it tears instead of stretching thin, according to Allrecipes. No set number of minutes or folds guarantees strong gluten, so a baker keeps kneading until the dough passes the stretch test. A recipe's kneading time is only an estimate based on someone else's dough. This step makes the plainest case for watching the dough instead of the clock, because the baker can see the result.

A dough that keeps tearing may reflect the mixing method rather than the baker's skill. The Gazette reports that gluten develops less in hand-mixed dough than in dough made with a mixer, and weak gluten is what makes dough tear. Abigail Lucas, lead instructor at We're Cookin' Now!, considers a stand mixer essential equipment, and she recommends one whose paddle has a silicone edge. A mixer may help a baker pass a test that keeps failing by hand, but it guarantees nothing. The windowpane still decides when the kneading is done, and a machine may only help the baker get there sooner.

The type of flour also affects how easily the gluten comes together. Stacey Ballis, who wrote the Allrecipes guide, says whole-grain flours make the job harder because they contain less natural gluten. A baker who works with whole-grain flour should therefore expect a tougher time getting the dough to pass the windowpane test. The effort at this stage also pays off at a step still to come, and Ballis said that "you will find the work at the early stages will help you tremendously when it comes to forming your eventual loaves."

Proofing is the stage when a baker sets the shaped dough aside and waits. Waiting can feel like doing nothing, so this is the step a hurried baker is most likely to cut short. Allrecipes calls underproofing an error that beginners make easily and that carries a high risk, and a baker who underproofs ends up with big tunnels or a heavy, damp crumb. A loaf like that is plainly wrong inside, not just a little bland. The trouble stays hidden under the crust, and the baker finds it only when slicing into the bread. By then the flour and the day are already spent, and nothing can fix it.

The fix is to wait longer than feels necessary, not to time the proof more precisely. When the dough proofs in the refrigerator, a full day is the starting point rather than the goal. "I use 24 hours as the bare minimum for a cold-proofed sourdough, which is super forgiving," Stacey Ballis wrote in the Allrecipes guide. A baker who holds the dough cold that long can miss the exact hour without harming the loaf. In proofing, the real danger is giving the dough too little time. The safer habit is to plan for a long, cold proof and stop treating a recipe's shortest time as the target.

A baker who leaves the dough in the fridge for a full day stops depending on exact timing and gains room for error.

Shaping can look like tidying up, a quick tuck and roll that makes the dough neat before it goes into the oven. Its real purpose is structural. When a baker shapes a loaf, the work pulls the outer layer of the dough taut, and loaves shaped without that surface tension spread flat, according to Allrecipes. Dough with nothing tight across its outside has no support to hold it upright, and it bakes wide and low instead of tall. Poor shaping therefore costs a loaf its form. That result is decided in a few minutes of handling, after the starter has matured and the dough has fermented. A baker can get every earlier stage right and still lose the loaf's height at this point.

No count of minutes or folds tells a baker when the shaping is done. The dough on the counter shows whether it worked, and the check is its posture. "Your formed dough should sit up a little proudly on the counter surface, and not slump," Stacey Ballis, who wrote the Allrecipes guide, said. A shaped round that holds its height has the tension it needs to stand in the oven. A round that sags and widens on the counter lacks that tension, and the baker can see the problem in plain view before any heat is applied. That check takes a few seconds and needs no thermometer, scale or timer.

A slumping round is a warning a baker can see and act on while the loaf is still raw, rather than discover once it comes out of the oven flat.

Before it goes into the oven, a sourdough loaf has to be slashed through its skin. In the heat the dough expands quickly and has to split open somewhere, and the baker's slash decides where. When the baker cuts too shallow or skips the cut, the loaf tears wherever its skin is weakest. A cut that is too shallow, or no cut at all, can make a loaf blow out and bake misshapen and dense, according to Allrecipes. The sign to watch is in the cut itself. Bakers should "score deeply enough to breach the tight outer surface and go a bit into the dough underneath," said Stacey Ballis, who wrote the Allrecipes guide. Half an inch is the depth to aim for, but it is a target rather than a minimum every loaf must reach. The purpose of the slash is to get through that taut surface, not to hit an exact number.

How much a weak score hurts the bread depends on the rest of the bake. A blowout or a dense loaf is not certain, though. Tasting Table reports that an unscored loaf keeps essentially the same taste and texture, as long as every other step was done right and the starter is active. A dense loaf after a botched cut may therefore point to an earlier fault, such as a sluggish starter. For a baker whose starter and proofing are sound, a missed slash mostly costs the loaf its shape and some of its height. That is a flaw in appearance, not a ruined bake. Careful scoring won't fix a loaf with other problems, and a careless cut leaves the baker no room for them.

Avoiding this mistake costs a beginner almost nothing. A beginner needs no elaborate design, and a plain X $TWTR or a square made from four slashes will serve, according to Tasting Table, while a Fenikara professional bread lame sells on Amazon $AMZN for under $7.

Get the blade through the skin with a cheap tool and a plain pattern, and save the worry for the steps that can ruin the crumb.

A baker who ends the bake too soon ruins the loaf outright, because the inside never finishes cooking. Allrecipes warns that underbaked loaves come out damp and doughy. The loaf can look ready on the outside and still be raw in the middle. A finished loaf reaches 200 degrees F inside, so the baker needs to check that temperature rather than count minutes. No set bake time stands in for that reading. The fix is to measure the loaf, not to add a few minutes to the timer and hope. A recipe's bake time only suggests when a baker should start checking. The temperature at the center of the loaf is the sign that the bread is done.

Checking the center takes a tool that many kitchens already have. With a meat thermometer, a baker can read the temperature inside the bread directly instead of guessing from the color of the crust. A baker without a thermometer can still check by hand with the thump test. Either way, no special equipment is required, and a baker who underbakes a loaf has skipped the check rather than lacked the means to make it. Once the reading or the test confirms the bread is done, the baker can take it out with confidence instead of by guesswork.

A doughy center undoes every hour spent on the starter and the dough, and a quick check of the loaf's center protects that work.

The urge to slice a loaf right away is strong, because fresh sourdough looks finished the moment it leaves the oven. Cooling is the final stage of baking, and slicing a loaf early harms its texture, according to Allrecipes. A baker who cuts too soon damages a loaf that every earlier step got right. Stacey Ballis, who wrote the Allrecipes guide, said the wait does real work: "The cooling and resting process with sourdough isn't there to prevent you burning your mouth on hot bread, it is actually the final step in the baking process." Scott Mitchell, a home baker in Bungendore, Australia, gave the same advice in 2018, according to ABC News. The bake ends when the loaf has cooled, not when the oven timer goes off.

A baker should wait until the loaf has cooled completely, rather than count off a set number of minutes. Wanting warm bread is no reason to cut early, because a baker who does things in the right order can have both. Ballis says to let the loaf cool completely first and then rewarm it for 10 minutes before serving. A baker can bring back the warmth in minutes, but no step can repair a texture ruined by an early cut.

This rule asks only for patience. A baker who waits gets both the texture and the warm slice, and one who cuts early gets the warmth and loses the texture.

Throwing out sourdough discard means losing an ingredient that every feeding produces and that bakers can still use. Many bakers treat discard as the cost of keeping a starter and scrape it straight into the trash. At each feeding, CBC Life reports, Soo Kim, a Toronto-based professional cook, baker and writer, scoops out 80 per cent of her starter, and she tops up the jar with flour and warm water in the same amount. Fresh ingredients go into the jar and much of it comes out again at the next feeding, so the leftovers keep piling up as long as the baker keeps the starter alive. Stacey Ballis, who wrote the Allrecipes guide, says she keeps her own leftover starter in the refrigerator, where it stays usable for extra bakes. A baker can't avoid making discard, and the only real decision is whether to pour it out. "The magic gloop that is your sourdough discard is a gift that keeps on giving, so don't let it go to waste," Ballis said.

Discard can go into meals from breakfast to dinner, and most of them aren't bread. Ballis turns hers into scones and bagels. CBC Life reports that Kim folds it into waffles, pancakes, biscuits, pizza dough and crackers. These recipes empty a real share of the jar, and a single batch of Kim's waffle or pancake mix uses up 1 cup of discard. A regular baker can plan a discard recipe each week the same way they plan a loaf, and the leftovers get eaten rather than stacking up.

Starter that sits unfed for a long stretch is slower to bounce back. Old, neglected starter needs a few days of refreshing before it is ready to bake with again, according to CBC Life. The sign to watch is the jar itself. When the stash at the back of the refrigerator keeps growing, it is time to bake with it, so it never becomes a forgotten jar that needs days of feeding.

A baker who uses the discard gets more food out of every feeding than the bread alone.

Most bakers treat a loaf as finished once it leaves the oven, but its eating life has only begun, and that life is short. Vanessa Kimbell of the Sourdough School in Northamptonshire, U.K., gives her boule about three days at its best, according to CBC. That is her guide for one recipe rather than a limit for every sourdough, but it shows how little time a baker has before the bread starts to decline. Good storage makes a loaf last longer, Allrecipes reports, and stale bread can still become croutons or crumbs. A storage mistake therefore wastes bread without ruining a bake, and the fix is to keep using the loaf instead of throwing it out.

The loaf's temperature tells a baker when to wrap it. Covering bread while it is still warm is the error to avoid, and once it has cooled a plain, dry kitchen towel is all the covering it needs, according to CBC. Freezing is another option for bread that won't be eaten soon, because a frozen loaf can be thawed and refreshed in the oven, according to Allrecipes. A baker who freezes a spare loaf doesn't have to give it up. The order matters more than the date. Wrap the loaf once it cools, freeze what you won't eat soon, and turn the last stale pieces into something new only at the very end, so that no loaf goes straight into the trash.

Bakers also store the culture that made the loaf. Between bakes, Kimbell keeps her starter in the fridge until it is time for the next loaf, according to CBC. Storing it well is the first step of the next bake.

Sourdough ends where it began, with a living culture in a jar, and the baker who reads that jar rather than the clock bakes the better bread.

How to be unemployed: Glenn Kelman on life after Redfin

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Glenn Kelman on Nov 6, 2026 at 12:47 pmNovember 6, 2026 at 12:52 pm

After 20 years at Redfin, and 10 months of unemployment, I’ve co-founded a new company. My business partner and I will announce our plans after we’ve built our product, but in the meantime GeekWire asked me to explain what I learned in the transition between Redfin and this new venture.

Many of us are in transition because society is in transition, with AI hollowing out big companies and making it easier to start new companies.

In the past, my own transitions had been harrowing, because I ran out of money or lost my sense of purpose. Leaving Redfin, I thought “here we go again.” I felt self-conscious being in the grocery store at 11 a.m. on a weekday. I was ashamed when I couldn’t come up with an idea for a company. But I made it to the other side.

To do that, I had to convince myself that I was an intrepid explorer, even if what I was exploring wasn’t Mount Everest, but instead what I could be. I wanted to feel brave about encounters I otherwise would’ve found embarrassing. Over the last year, I met people about becoming a high school teacher, a Whistler bagel shopkeeper, a candidate for the state legislature, the founder of a tree-planting nonprofit, the CEO of a pre-IPO company and a project leader for Sound Transit.

The way to prepare yourself to do one thing for a long time is first to consider everything else.

At the beginning of my exploration, a CEO recruiter told me the startup scene in Seattle was “dead” and that, for all of Seattle’s talent, more companies were forming in New York and even LA. I Googled “high-growth Seattle startups” and the list included one company that went bankrupt in 2023, and Redfin, which was acquired in 2025.

Another friend told me that she was on a Discord channel with a bunch of CTOs who were miserable trying to do “AI transformations” at businesses with legacy codebases. At least for me, the logical response to all this has been to start an AI-native company in Seattle.

“The way to prepare yourself to do one thing for a long time is first to consider everything else.”

It took me months to feel ready to do that. In January, a friend told me I wasn’t allowed to make any big decisions until September. Another said I had to wait until the frenzy of my last job had subsided. “You’ll know it has happened,” he said, “because food will start tasting better” (it never happened).

At a conference, a fashion entrepreneur who’d built and abandoned vast residences in Jackson Hole and Costa Rica, and got divorced twice in the past three years, gave me a bracelet that said “we are enough.”

Since I’d often been thinking I hadn’t done enough in the morning to deserve lunch, it was a relief to feel that I didn’t have to be creative or productive in order to justify my existence. But relieving that pressure also made it easier to be creative and productive.

On a call walking through Volunteer Park, I told a friend that the bracelet reminded me I could be happy and never start a company again. She said, “YOU JUST NEED A CO-FOUNDER.”

She told me to share flawed ideas with people who could make them better. I set up meetings with potential co-founders, and we shared our ideas before they were ready, because our meetings had become deadlines. Then we used AI to turn our ideas into products, so we could see what was good and bad about each idea. Starting a company started to feel like a natural progression, instead of Athena sprouting out of Zeus’s head.

When I confessed to a friend that everything we were working on still seemed so small, she said, “What about breakfast burritos?” She outlined her vision for a shop serving breakfast burritos all day, with a line out the door. Then she started talking about inventing a nursing home “that makes everyone happy.” Rather than running a McKinsey market-sizing analysis, she just thought about what she wanted to exist in the world.

That’s something almost anyone can do. In her living room, I suddenly felt that humanity would always have an instinct — this is a bizarre word, but it’s what popped into my head — to fructify. I remember walking home from her house with my wife and hearing the thick sound of insects humming in the night as proof of life’s rich pageant. This is what I mean by surrounding yourself with good vibes.

The last step in my process maybe should’ve come first, choosing where I wanted to make a difference. I thought about all the companies that I honestly would’ve been proud to start, and they were all in the same industry. I had no experience in that industry, but a friend said I could offset that if I were willing to give it everything I have, which is what I’m now doing. I met amazing, generous investors who gave me a good idea, and a business partner who helped me think it through.

Through all of this, I depended on my wife more than any husband ever should. She works herself to the bone treating people with cancer, but she took my calls in the middle of her workday, when I would talk in detail about what to cook for dinner and then — one more thing! — what’s the meaning of my life?

Once, she was getting into our Chevy Bolt for her morning commute while I was rounding the corner with the dog, and I saw her brighten in the way many people did back when I was Redfin’s CEO.

I found myself full of anticipation. She reached into the car, as if to grab a small gift, and heaved something at me from across the street, saying it “needs reprogramming.” Before I realized it was a garage clicker, I thought she had said I was the one who needed reprogramming. “Maybe that’s true,” I said to myself, trudging past the browned lawn, and the dirty dishes, and into a world of possibilities.

In just a few weeks, AWS re:Invent brings advanced and expert-level sessions on the services you build on every day, from serverless and containers to databases and developer tools. Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

Buttermilk Drop Biscuits

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Recipe by Grace Vallo, Windham, New Hampshire

These buttermilk drop biscuits are tender, fluffy and buttery—no rolling, kneading or shaping required.

Buttermilk biscuits are, in my humble opinion, the perfect breakfast. They’re endlessly versatile—perfect for drowning in spicy sausage gravy, loading up with bacon and egg for a sandwich, or simply slathering with butter and jam. There’s really nothing a flaky biscuit can’t do. But I don’t always have the time or energy to roll and cut dough in the morning, and that’s where drop biscuits come in to save the day—or at least to stop me from opening up a can of refrigerated dough.

Making drop biscuits is almost improbably easy. You just stir together a few pantry staples to make the dough, plop spoonfuls on the pan and bake them until they’re perfectly golden brown. Whether you’re an experienced biscuit maker or looking to whip up your first-ever batch, this simple recipe delivers maximum payoff with just a couple of simple ingredients and a little bit of effort.

Preheat the oven to 425°F. As the oven warms up, combine the flour, baking soda, baking powder and salt in a large mixing bowl.

Editor’s Tip: Use a fork or a whisk to give the ingredients a good stir to make sure they’re all evenly incorporated.

Using a pastry cutter or a fork, cut the chilled butter into the dry ingredients until the mixture resembles coarse crumbs.

Editor’s Tip: You could use your hands to incorporate the butter into the flour, but use a light touch. The heat from your hands can melt the butter, and you don’t want that!

Stir the buttermilk into the crumb mixture just until it’s moistened.

Editor’s Tip: Grab your buttermilk straight from the refrigerator so it’s nice and cold. Mix just until you’ve formed a shaggy dough. It’s OK if your dough looks a little bit lumpy—you don’t want to over-mix it!

Drop the dough by 1/4-cupfuls 2 inches apart onto a greased baking sheet. Bake the biscuits until they’re golden brown on top, 11 to 13 minutes. Serve warm with butter, if desired.

Editor’s Tip: Don’t worry about making them perfectly round. A little bit of irregularity is part of the fun of drop biscuits.

Drop biscuits tend to be best right out of the oven, but you can place any leftovers inside an airtight storage container, where they’ll last on the counter for three to five days. Heat the biscuits up in a 200° oven for 5 to 10 minutes to bring them back to life.

There are several buttermilk substitutes, and the easiest one—white vinegar—is probably already in your pantry. Add 1 tablespoon of white vinegar to a 1-cup measure, then fill it the rest of the way with cold milk.

Make it a 3-ingredient recipe by swapping self-rising flour for the all-purpose flour. You won’t need the baking powder or the baking soda, as they’re already included in the flour mix. Mix the flour with the butter and buttermilk as directed to create an even easier batch of drop biscuits.

If your buttermilk drop biscuits are dense, check your baking powder. It’s possible that it’s expired, which could mean that it was too weak to help your dough rise. You may have also overworked the dough, which can result in a dense, tough biscuit.

Weezer, Hilary Duff and More Set for Billboard Live Music Summit 2026: WHAT TO EXPECT

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Billboard Live Music Summit to feature star-studded panels, awards, and luncheon sponsored by AEG Presents.

This article was created in partnership with AEG Presents.

Billboard’s Live Music Summit returns to Los Angeles on Nov. 10, bringing together some of the biggest names and influential voices in live entertainment for a day of industry conversations, networking and celebration. Among this year’s honorees are Weezer, receiving the Legends of Live Award, and Hilary Duff, recognized with Outstanding Pop Tour of the Year. With more than three decades of touring under their belt, Weezer has built a devoted, multigenerational fanbase, bringing their catalog of alternative rock anthems to stages around the world. Currently on their 32-city arena, The Gathering World Tour, the band continues to demonstrate the enduring appeal that has made them a force in live music.

At the summit, Weezer will reflect on their touring career and lasting impact on the industry in a conversation with Billboard co-chief content officer Jason Lipshutz. Beyond the stage, the summit offers opportunities for the people behind live music to connect. Returning as a sponsor, AEG Presents will host this year’s luncheon, bringing artists, executives and industry leaders together for an afternoon of conversation and networking. As a leading global live entertainment company behind major tours, festivals and live experiences, AEG Presents shares the summit’s commitment to celebrating the people and partnerships that keep the industry moving.

Hilary Duff will also take the spotlight, receiving the Outstanding Pop Tour of the Year honor for her Lucky Me Tour and joining Billboard correspondent Lyndsey Havens for an exclusive conversation. Rounding out the programming, Billboard’s co-chief content officer Leila Cobo will moderate a conversation on international touring with OCESA’s Guillermo “Memo” Parra, Live Nation’s Nidhish Varughese, Oak View Group’s Steve Collins, and AEG Presents’ Angie Rho.

8 Reasons a Contractor Might Give You an Outrageously High Estimate

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you just got a quote for a home repair that made your eyes water, there's probably a reason it's so high. A team of contractors weighs in with possible explanations.

If you just got a quote from a contractor that was way more than you thought it would be, join the club…it happens to the best of us. In fact, it’s a pretty common occurrence. Sometimes, though, the quote is so high that you can feel your mouth forming words you shouldn’t be saying. As someone who has been on both sides of such interactions, I can say that contractors don’t like them any more than you do. They know you can shop around, and they need your business. So what’s the deal with ridiculously high quotes?

I didn’t find any shortage of contractors willing to weigh in on this topic — one that is near and dear to their professional hearts. If a quote just made you hyperventilate, take a deep breath and check out some of the possible reasons why it’s so much higher than you expected.

“If an HVAC unit has a defective part that needs replacement, most of my clients will immediately ask how much the replacement part costs,” says HVAC technician Tamer Sayed. “I always tell the current market price and the misunderstanding often starts here. A few clients forget to take into account the labor and consultation fees. They assume they only had to pay for the replacement part.”

The same thing can happen with plumbing and electrical jobs. The part needed to get things working again may be small and inexpensive, and it may only account for a small portion of the quote. Sometimes the labor quote seems excessive, and that’s usually because the client doesn’t understand what’s involved with the repair.

Jake Vincett, a builder from the UK, makes this observation: “A lot of the time, the customer is comparing quotes for different work, because the cheaper one has included a surface repair whereas the more expensive one has quoted for fixing the underlying problem…It’s common that the customer doesn’t know or consider the amount of work going into a project. A new driveway isn’t just laying tarmac – it’s digging out the old one, laying the sub base, etc.”

Plumbing, electrical and HVAC expert Bob Cardwell adds: “It’s often not only one problem to fix but a bunch of small fixes. Even though it might be possible to fix the original problem without addressing the additional issues, an experienced and reliable technician will not ignore them, will try to fix them and will discuss them with the homeowner.”

Failing to provide a quote that addresses underlying issues and then delivering a sky-high bill at the end doesn’t just make the customer unhappy, says HVAC and plumbing expert Ryan Osterkamp. “It can make them feel like we don’t know what we’re doing.”

Osterkamp is used to hearing customers say: “I can go get that part for $X at Home Depot.” What they aren’t thinking about is the amount of training and expertise that made it possible for our technician to quickly and accurately diagnose the issue, have the supplies they need in their truck and be able to do the repair same day. Sure, you may save money by going with the guy on Facebook that works out of an old, unlabeled truck, but if his work doesn’t hold up, will he fix it without giving you the runaround? Is he insured? Does he offer a warranty?”

Tariffs, inflation and the economy in general are pushing prices up, and the dollar has lost a lot of its purchasing power for the average American. The same is true for contractors. “The fact of the matter is, we’re working harder now and making less,” says remodeling contractor Kevin Cullen. “In today’s economy, a lot of people are chasing the price to the bottom and shopping for the cheapest number. Unfortunately, they usually get what they pay for.”

A contractor who knows problems might creep up, such as a rotten subfloor that needs replacement during a toilet repair, makes an effort to warn customers as part of the quote. Potential problems aren’t always obvious. “There are times,” says Nika Tveretinova, who operates an HVAC business in Sacramento, “when the actual fix isn’t that complicated, but it’s the access that makes things tough—especially with equipment installed in an attic or cramped quarters. Sometimes we also find an old installation that needs a safety or code correction before we can put everything back together.”

In such situations, a higher-than-expected quote that you can reject is far better than a higher-than-expected bill you have to pay because the work is already completed.

Joshua Kurz founded NumeralQ, a home improvement pricing guide, and he cautions against comparing a bid you receive from a contractor to information you find online. “Owners often find an estimate online, and rather than waiting for a bid, they assume that number will be the actual cost. These estimates are helpful when determining a budget, but they are not interchangeable for actual bids. A 1,500 sq ft roof in one market will not necessarily cost the same in another market, and bids can even differ for neighboring houses.”

If you find a guy on Facebook who charges a low price to do a repair, there’s no guarantee that the repair will last or be up to code. When that person makes a mistake, a licensed contractor working on the same system at a later date might have to fix it, especially when inspectors are involved.

“Over a 40-year career,” says Cullen, “I’ve found many surprises: unsupported headers, no king or jack studs, undersized headers, poorly installed headers that aren’t flush and have gaps, termite damage, carpenter ant damage, water damage, mold. That’s not every job, but you never know when a homeowner bought a house in which a Rube Goldberg did the work on it.”

Sometimes a house is in such poor condition that a small repair, such as stopping a plumbing leak, can turn into a major project that involves replacing pipes, walls or subfloors. Rather than simply declining the repair, some contractors will offer a ridiculously high quote to avoid falling into a sinkhole of extra work. If the customer accepts the quote, the contractor won’t lose money by taking the job. A customer who rejects a bid can keep shopping with no harm done.

I Thought Multi-Tools Were a Gimmick — Then I Took One on a Camping Trip

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If, like me, you think multi-tools are gimmicky gadgets, you might change your mind once you discover how handy they are when your toolbox isn’t within reach.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

I admit I scoffed when my partner first started raving about his Leatherman Wave+ multi-tool. I thought it looked like an expensive, over-elaborate gadget that was destined to gather dust in a junk drawer.

However, as a keen hiker and camper, I’ve discovered how useful it is to have pliers, knives, a saw, scissors, files and other tools packed into one compact, quality piece of kit. I’ve also watched my tech-obsessed partner regularly reach for its precision tools when tinkering with his electrical equipment.

Ahead, I’ll share how a tool I once dismissed as a gimmick earned a permanent spot in my pack, and what two experts think makes a Leatherman worth the price tag.

I had to realize that our Leatherman Wave+ isn’t meant to replace a well-stocked toolbox to understand its value. Instead, it fills the gap when dedicated tools aren’t conveniently available or really needed.

At home, I regularly use the tool’s tiny scissors to carefully prune dead leaves from our many houseplants, the knife to open packages and the small screwdriver to tighten the legs on my glasses. My partner is an IT professional who also has an impressive collection of synthesizers, and he regularly reaches for the tool to make small technical repairs and splice wires.

I’ve found the Wave+ even more useful when we’re borrowing my parents’ campervan, hiking, or camping. The bottle opener is handy for those after-adventure beers, the serrated knife slices baguettes for trailside sandwiches, the saw cuts small, dry branches for kindling, and the screwdriver has adjusted our portable stove and tightened folding chairs.

The pocket-sized multi-tool packs 18 functions into a stainless-steel form that measures just four inches when closed and weighs 8.5 ounces. Alongside the tools I’ve mentioned, it has pliers, bit drivers, a wire stripper and crimper, a ruler, an extra knife blade and file, and an admittedly slightly-tricky-to-use can opener.

While I don’t regularly use all 18 tools, and I might never use some, having the selection provides peace of mind, especially in remote places.

Daniel Kilburn, founder of Emergency Action Planning and a preparedness expert, has carried his Leatherman Surge since his military service in 2006. “It’s an expedient tool to have on hand when you know you’re going to be someplace where you won’t have access to any others,” he says.

Scott Emanuel of knives, multi-tools, and outdoor equipment retailer Heinnie Haynes is also a multi-tool convert. He appreciated having one for quick fixes while renovating his cottage, and particularly rates the needle-nose pliers, which rival standard models rather than acting as an emergency substitute.

Kilburn suggests multi-tools can be a useful starting point for someone who doesn’t own a toolbox, and at the other end of the spectrum are the tinkerers and prepared problem-solvers Emanuel sees as natural fits.

“The person who will value a Leatherman is the one who spends their after-hours in the garage building stuff that they probably never need, it’s the kind of self-reliant and prepared person who you rely on to solve a problem,” Emanuel says.

The Leatherman Wave+ currently costs $129.95, meaning it should be a well-considered purchase rather than an impulse buy.

It’s reassuring to know that you’ll be investing in a refined product designed because of a genuine practical need. Founder Tim Leatherman conceived a pliers-based multi-tool after encountering car and plumbing problems while traveling through Europe in the 1970s, and after careful development, he launched the original Pocket Survival Tool in 1983.

Today you’re paying for that established reputation and durable construction, and Leatherman backs its products with a 25-year warranty.

“They’re ridiculously well built, really well regarded, and their choice of tools is good,” Emanuel says. He describes sought-after Leathermans as the “collectible top end of the multi-tool market.”

Kilburn recalls seeing lower-priced multi-tools failing his comrades during their military career. “Some disintegrate over time because they were just cheaply made with poor-quality material [and] poor-quality steel,” he says. His Leatherman Surge, meanwhile, has lasted over 20 years. If replacing it again, he says he would choose Leatherman over his backup Gerber multi-tool because he prefers its feel and durability.

Leatherman makes a wide range of multi-tools, so it pays to consider which functions you’ll genuinely use. The Signal model adds outdoor-focused features suited to camping, including a ferrocerium rod, emergency whistle and sharpener, but omits scissors. I use scissors enough for plant pruning, first aid and gear repairs that the Wave+ remains the better fit for me.

If you’re unlikely to use most of its functions, a smaller, cheaper multi-tool could make more sense. The Wave+ is also heavier than minimalist models, and there’s a learning curve when figuring out where everything lives.

Don’t forget that a premium multi-tool only represents good value if you look after it. We remove dirt after outdoor adventures and dry and lubricate it before storing. We also avoid forcing it to tackle jobs better suited to full-size tools. I wouldn’t, for example, choose its compact saw over a dedicated full-size version for pruning substantial tree branches in my yard.

If you’re using a Wave+ in the UK, like me, be aware of the country’s knife laws. You need a “good reason” to carry it in public because its blades lock. However, Emanuel explains that the legal language is ambiguous, so you shouldn’t assume you can carry one wherever you go just because it’s part of a multi-tool.

The Wave+ has convinced me that a good multi-tool is more than merely an expensive gimmick. It may not replace dedicated tools, but having so many functions at my fingertips in a form that is so compact, portable and easy to use makes it a practical, worthwhile investment.

The Wave Plus is a multi-tool workhorse. It packs 18 tools in one, including sharp blades, spring scissors, wire cutters, and more.

US Treasury walks back crypto surveillance ‘travel rule’ proposal

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on funds worth $250 or more.

The Treasury Department has withdrawn a controversial proposal that would have expanded reporting requirements for cryptocurrency transactions.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on transactions of $250 or more, decreasing that threshold from $3,000.

In a filing, FinCEN said it was withdrawing the proposed rule because more than five years had passed, the convertible virtual currency industry had changed and additional public input would be helpful.

"The Agencies believe that any future, potential amendments to the Recordkeeping Rule and Travel Rule related to CVC should be considered in light of these recent developments to ensure that such amendments are fit for purpose," FinCEN said.

When the rule was proposed, FinCEN had argued that bad actors were increasingly using smaller transactions to move illicit funds. However, crypto groups, like Coin Center, had warned that lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 would mean that more ordinary transactions would be subject to "warrantless financial surveillance."

In a blog on Friday, Coin Center said it was happy to see the rule proposal rescinded, along with another rule proposal that was withdrawn last month that would have imposed reporting and recordkeeping requirements on transactions involving self-hosted wallets.

"This follows Treasury’s withdrawal last month of the proposed 'unhosted wallet' and cryptocurrency mixing rules, both of which Coin Center had also opposed," the organization said. 'Taken together, these withdrawals close the books on several long-pending proposals that would have expanded the Bank Secrecy Act’s already extensive financial surveillance regime."

Crypto Council for Innovation CEO Ji Hun Kim said in a statement that withdrawing the travel rule was the "right call."

"It had not moved forward over the past several years for good reason," he said. "Lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 for cross-border transfers would have placed undue burden on institutions."

The Block's news, research and explainers are reported, written and edited by our editorial team. We sometimes use AI tools for data analysis, summaries or AI overviews. An editor checks the facts, figures and quotes they produce against primary sources, and every piece is reviewed by an editor before it is published. AHA to Put Aggressive BP Control in the Hot Seat for SPRINT-Excluded Groups

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

— Statins, inflammation- and lipoprotein(a)-lowering therapies also featured at meeting

At this weekend's American Heart Association (AHA) meeting, intensive blood pressure (BP) lowering and other mainstays in cardiovascular medicine have a lot to prove, as do innovations related to GLP-1 receptor agonists and artificial intelligence (AI).

AHA's main scientific sessions run from Saturday, Nov. 7 to Monday, Nov. 9, with expanded programming starting on Friday, Nov. 6. The meeting will be held in person in Chicago and with livestreaming of select sessions.

Featured among the first AHA late-breakers will be Saturday's OPTIMAL-Stroke and OPTIMAL-Diabetes, two separate trials of intensive BP control that are expected to fill major gaps left by the landmark SPRINT trial from over 10 years ago.

While both OPTIMAL studies tested a systolic BP target of <120 mmHg for the purpose of cardiovascular protection in people starting at 130-180 mmHg, the former sought ischemic stroke survivors in particular, and the latter higher-risk individuals with type 2 diabetes, both important patient groups excluded from the SPRINT trial that had contributed to the national redefining of hypertension in 2017.

Meanwhile, another cornerstone -- albeit controversial -- cardiovascular therapy has a major hurdle in STREAM, a Saturday afternoon late-breaker. This randomized noninferiority trial is larger than the prior SITE/SAGA and will provide more important evidence on whether statin users, older adults without cardiovascular disease, are put at risk if they discontinue the therapy.

Also in the hot seat is the familiar concept of an early invasive strategy of coronary stenting or bypass surgery in patients with stable ischemic heart disease. Years after the neutral reports of ISCHEMIA, ISCHEMIA-CKD, and COURAGE, these landmark trials have the opportunity to show whether the story changes with longer follow-up during a Saturday morning featured science session at AHA.

Drugs in development also feature heavily in the scientific program.

Among the most important stories will be whether there is still a path forward for inflammation- and lipoprotein(a)-lowering for cardiovascular benefit. In fact, the ZEUS cardiovascular outcomes report for the interleukin-6 blocker ziltivekimab and the Lp(a)HORIZON trial of lipoprotein(a)-lowering pelacarsen -- both with disappointing topline results publicly available for months now -- were chosen for the opening late-breaking trial session at AHA.

In electrophysiology, highlights include Sunday presentations of the OptimPacing trial of left bundle branch pacing versus right ventricular pacing for atrioventricular block, and the SUPERIOR trial of sacubitril/valsartan (Entresto) for preventing recurrent atrial fibrillation after catheter ablation in patients with hypertension.

Monday morning then brings the placebo-controlled ATTAIN-OSA late-breaking trial on using the GLP-1 receptor agonist orforglipron (Foundayo) in people with obstructive sleep apnea and obesity or overweight.

As for a more accessible way to check for left ventricular systolic function, smartwatches with AI-enabled ECGs present an innovative approach and are the subject of the WATCH-LV trial in AHA's closing late-breaking science session on Monday.

Binance hack is ‘cyber war,’ TRM Labs executive says

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

TRM Labs executive Ari Redbord called for governments to take offensive action against North Korean hackers following the record $6.3 billion Binance theft.

The $6.3 billion Binance hack sent shockwaves through the crypto industry. Following a series of increasingly severe cybersecurity incidents, the attack on the world’s biggest crypto exchange demonstrated how far state-backed hackers can, and will, go.

TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence company, was on the front lines of efforts to stop the theft, releasing a report in its aftermath. The firm was early to attribute the attack to the North Korean hacking group, TraitorTrader, citing “indisputable” onchain and offchain evidence. The $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025 was attributed to the same group.

Binance said in its postmortem on Nov. 3 that attackers posed as recruiters to plant malware on an engineer’s work computer, then used a compromised internal AI tool to reach its wallet-signing service.

Ari Redbord, TRM Labs’ global head of policy and government affairs, told Cointelegraph that although the industry’s coordinated response to the attack was unprecedented, “we are only going to see much more of this.”

The speed and scale of industry’s response to the hack were among the factors that stood out to Redbord.

Tether froze 971.1 million USDt (USDT) tokens just 37 minutes after the main sweep. Combined, stablecoin issuers froze tokens worth about $2.67 billion, according to TRM. “Nothing like that has ever been done before,” Redbord said. “They were able to claw back 40% right out the gate.”

The race to catch stolen funds before hackers can move them offchain is a critical part of defending against such attacks. TRM’s Redbord explained that the Beacon Network, an intelligence-sharing system TRM launched in 2025, allowed companies to coordinate quickly to immobilize the funds before working to seize them. “Normally I see a few Beacon alerts maybe every day. I’ve seen, I don’t know, hundreds or maybe even a thousand Beacon alerts over the last few days,” he said.

Related: Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

The push for coordination comes as Bloomberg reported on Nov. 4 that the T3 Financial Crime Unit, which TRM formed with Tron and Tether in September 2024, disbanded in late August, with sources citing slow responses and poor coordination. Tether said it would continue working with TRM through Beacon.

Although Redbord called for greater coordination, he warned that such efforts may do little to deter future attacks. He noted that North Korea’s Oct. 29 theft was equivalent to roughly a third of the country’s GDP and accounted for half of the $12.5 billion stolen by state-backed hackers since 2017. Even if attackers recover only $1 billion, the potential payoff remains enormous, he argued.

“North Korea will keep doing this as long as it’s effective,” he said.

Redbord argued that stopping such attacks requires offensive action from governments in response to North Korea’s use of hackers operating as an arm of the state.

TRM’s capacity gap report found that the gap between attackers and cybersecurity defenders continues to widen, driven by hackers’ early adoption of frontier technologies such as AI. He said law enforcement and compliance teams need AI to find and patch vulnerabilities as quickly as hackers can exploit them.

“Every time North Korea hacks, we should be hacking them back,” he continued, calling for offensive cyber operations and action against laundering networks and banks that hackers use to cash out. “This is cyber war,” he said.

Honoring Agnieszka Holland, Thierry Frémaux and Irish animation studio Cartoon Saloon, the Seville European Film Festival bows Friday with a main competition featuring Dani de la Orden’s “The Director” and Andrew Haigh’s “A Long Winter” and the ambition to capture the genius, true gamut and new talents of Europe’s productions.

Of Seville’s three 2026 honorees, Holland went first. BYD is launching this 5-seat luxury electric SUV next week with over 1,000 km of range

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

BYD’s luxury brand, Denza, calls it “the king of large 5-seater pure electric vehicles.” The new Denza N8 electric SUV will launch next week, offering an impressive CLTC driving range of over 1,000 km (621 miles), massive digital screens, and plenty of premium extras.

After opening pre-orders for the new 5-seat luxury electric SUV last week, Li Hui, Denza’s general manager, said the results so far “have been excellent.”

According to Li, the Denza N8 is “definitely the most anticipated luxury SUV of the year.” Using BYD’s second-generation Blade Batteries, the Denza N8 EV has a pure electric CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) on a single charge.

That’s just as much as the N8 plug-in hybrid (PHEV) it’s replacing, which offered a combined CLTC range of 1,003 km (623 miles) on a full tank of gas and a full charge.

While Li confirmed last week that the new EV variant would go on sale in November, he didn’t give a specific date.

Now, it’s official. Denza announced on its Weibo page on Friday that the launch event for the Denza N8 EV will be held on November 12, 2026.

BYD is still offering a limited-time promo that includes a 5,000 RMB credit for those who place a reservation with a 2,000 RMB ($200) deposit.

The Denza N8 measures 5,150 mm long, 1,999 mm wide, and 1,820 mm high, with a 3,075 mm wheelbase. That’s slightly longer than the Kia EV9 and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

It’s available with a single rear-wheel-drive (RWD) or a tri-motor all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain. The RWD version uses a single motor producing either 320 kW (429 hp) or 370 kW (496 hp).

The tri-motor Denza N8 uses BYD’s e3 platform. The setup includes three electric motors: a 270 kW (362 hp) front motor and two 310 kW (416 hp) rear motors, for a combined output of 890 kW (1,193 hp).

Regulatory filings earlier this year revealed it will use either a 130.15 kWh or 105.79 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery, offering a CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) and 800 km (497 miles), respectively.

The Denza N8 also features BYD’s latest Flash Charging technology, letting it recharge from 10% to 80% in just five minutes. It takes just nine minutes to go from 10% to 97%.

Inside, the five-seat electric SUV features a massive 30″ infotainment screen that spans nearly the entire passenger-side dashboard, plus a new 43″ (1.1 meter) panoramic head-up display (PHUD) at the bottom of the windshield.

Like most of its new luxury vehicles, the Denza N8 is loaded with BYD’s latest software and technology.

Added premium features include zero-gravity seating in all rows, a three-spoke steering wheel, wireless phone chargers, and ambient lighting.

The electric SUV is equipped with a roof-mounted LiDAR to support BYD’s God’s Eye 5.0 intelligent driving assistance system, plus its DiSus-A intelligent air suspension system.

BYD has yet to announce prices for the Denza N8 EV, but it will likely be slightly less than the N8L, an extended version that starts at 299,800 RMB ($45,000).

AI is dramatically accelerating AI research

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This post discusses a public METR report on AI R&D acceleration at Anthropic. I did not work on this project or see the unredacted report.1 All opinions are my own and do not represent METR or the report authors.Thanks to Ryan Greenblatt for discussion, feedback, and help with modeling, and Daniel Kokotajlo, Eli Lifland, and Hjalmar Wjik for comments.

Earlier this week, Anthropic stated in their S1 that they “may imminently meet” the threshold of “dramatically accelerated progress due to AI” as defined in their Responsible Scaling Policy. In their latest RSP (effective July 2026), say this threshold will be met when:

They observe or expect overall capabilities progress to be going twice as fast as the fastest sustained rate before AI was contributing much to AI R&D.2

This is plausibly attributable to AI automating AI R&D, rather than acceleration in compute or other inputs.3

For the last few months, some of my colleagues at METR have been working on a deep-access investigation to understand how much Anthropic is being accelerated by its AI systems. From my reading of their report and Anthropic’s RSP, it looks like the “dramatic acceleration” threshold has already been crossed.4

Between October 2025 and October 2026, capabilities progress at Anthropic has been going about twice as fast as it had been over the previous twelve months. The measurement they report is an internal version of the Epoch Capabilities Index called AECI; a wide range of other supporting evidence for acceleration has been redacted. They said that this redacted evidence was "a majority of the evidence [they] found most informative."

While the pace may have initially started picking up for a mix of reasons, AI automation is probably the large majority of the reason why capabilities are still improving at this faster pace today. Their reasons for believing this have been redacted.

This is a much bigger deal than it might sound at first. I think this report is the most important update we have gotten about AI risk in the last couple of years — much bigger than the Hugging Face attack. Naively, it points toward much shorter timelines than most external researchers (including the AI 2027 team) have projected.

Importantly, if AI is roughly doubling capabilities progress overall, it must be speeding up human researchers by much more than that.

Researchers work on improving algorithms and architectures; Anthropic has sometimes referred to these innovations as “compute multipliers.”5 Overall AI capabilities (“effective compute”) is the product of compute multipliers and training compute. Because the compute multipliers are only one input into capabilities, this means that if AI capabilities are advancing twice as fast overall because of software progress, the rate of growth in compute multipliers would need to be more than twice as fast: perhaps ~2.5-3x faster based on reasonable assumptions.6 Since there’s uncertainty about the size of speedup and how much of it comes from AI, I’ll go with ~2-3x.

Similarly, researcher labor is only one input to discovering these compute multipliers: they need to test their ideas, which requires compute for experiments. If we assume a very simple Cobb-Douglas model where experiment compute and labor are similarly important, that means the uplift to labor roughly needs to be the square of the acceleration in compute multipliers.7 This would imply that we would need a (~2-3x)^2 = ~4-9x speed up in labor to make overall AI capabilities go twice as fast.8

However, once capabilities start speeding up significantly, this also means that low-hanging fruit is plucked at a correspondingly faster rate. Each new compute multiplier takes more and more work to discover and implement (i.e., “ideas are getting harder to find”). Before there was much acceleration from AI, labor roughly grew at some roughly fixed exponential rate from hiring more humans. Combined with growth in compute, this compensated for diminishing returns enough to maintain the baseline rate of capabilities progress. But to maintain a new faster rate of capabilities progress without scaling compute faster, we would need a new faster rate of growth in overall labor.

Since the human labor force is stuck growing at more or less the old rate, the uplift from AI would need to get larger and larger over time just to sustain capabilities progress at its new rate. That means the uplift today, after several months of accelerated progress from AI,9 needs to be even higher than ~4-9x, the estimate for what it would take to double progress at the start: maybe the necessary labor uplift is ~10x. This is very uncertain, and substantially more or less is plausible. But it lines up surprisingly well with informal self-reports from Anthropic staff, who say they are now getting a ~12x uplift from the use of internal models.

When you’re picturing a 10x uplift, you should not be imagining that every researcher is now a team of ten researchers — as anyone who has ever done anything knows, a team of ten does not get ten times as much valuable work done per hour as one person. Instead, it’s as if every researcher at Anthropic is in a time warp bubble where they experience ten days for every day that passes in the outside world. As if AI lets them think, read, speak, type, and do everything else ten times faster.10 Anthropic probably wouldn’t have been able to speed up its research this much by hiring any number of people.11

A reasonable default expectation is that as AI capabilities continue to improve, AI researchers will be sped up more and more from automation: if today it takes them a month to get as much work done as they used to in a year, soon it will take a week, and then perhaps a day. If this increasing labor uplift is strong enough to overcome diminishing returns, then in the absence of other bottlenecks it would translate into even larger speed ups in the rate of AI progress: progress will start going 4x faster, and then 8x, and then 16x, and so on.

The report authors were very uncertain about this question, and say that their picture changed several times with new information. But ultimately, they did not find evidence of bottlenecks or steep diminishing returns that would get in the way of compounding acceleration.12 They also report seeing some (confusing) redacted evidence indicating that acceleration could already be above 2x and increasing over time.

If we take it at face value that AI is now nearly doubling the rate of AI capabilities progress and assume acceleration from AI is smooth, this naively implies that we would reach extreme superintelligence within a year. It seems that around a year’s worth of AI progress got us from modest acceleration to the point where AIs now make AI progress roughly twice as fast. If acceleration from AI is smoothly increasing, then at this new rate, we should expect that we will double the rate of progress again within six months. At that point, it would take three months to double it again, then six weeks.13

In theory, these doubling times would add up to infinite capabilities within one year. In reality, at some point we would reach the effective ceiling on software efficiency. But I expect these limits to be very far from where we are now.

If we go through this feedback loop, I expect that the AI systems that come out the other side will be able to very quickly develop novel weapons and very easily overpower human civilization in a conflict. If these systems were seriously misaligned, I think they would likely disempower humanity, and this could lead to human extinction.

The “dramatically accelerated progress due to AI” is such an important and extreme milestone because it is a strong concrete indicator of extreme capabilities in the very near term. Indeed, in an earlier version of Anthropic’s RSP (May 2025), the highest named risk threshold for AI research automation (AI R&D-5) corresponded to a somewhat weaker milestone.14 According to the old RSP, Anthropic would be required to have, at a minimum, security good enough to “protect against model-weight theft by state-level adversaries”15 by the time it reached AI R&D-5.

I agree that very strong security, both for model weights and algorithmic secrets themselves, is a minimum prerequisite for undergoing an intelligence explosion next year without running very large risks of killing billions of people. The AI industry is nowhere near this bar: both OpenAI and Anthropic recently had major security breaches by amateur groups. Beyond strong model weight security, I also think we would need robust logging and monitoring with universal coverage, reliable measurements of monitorability, effective countermeasures against monitor collusion, principled alignment training techniques with very convincing model organisms or interpretability to understand how well they’re working, governance infrastructure for pacing progress if we notice these alignment and control interventions start to break down…and many other measures if we’re going to approach an intelligence explosion in anything like a responsible way.

Unfortunately, most of the important evidence that fed into these conclusions has been redacted from this report. Analytical choices will inevitably be fraught, and the report authors say that the evidence was often confusing.

I trust that my colleagues did a careful and rigorous job navigating making sense of what they saw, and I made a big update based on this report. But I wish much more of the underlying evidence was transparent so readers don’t have to rely on trust. We may be running out of time to get on the same page about timelines and takeoff speeds, and it is much harder to do that when external scientists cannot examine the data and test out their own models.

Because I may have seen other confidential information that might be relevant to timelines and takeoff speeds, I will only discuss implications of public evidence rather than sharing precise bottom line forecasts.

The precise condition is: “We observe or expect double the rate of progress in AI aggregate capabilities compared to both the rate we’d expect and the fastest rate of extended progress we’ve observed in the absence of significant AI contributions to AI R&D.” My understanding is the reason to have both of these conditions (double the rate of progress both compared to the counterfactual and compared to the fastest sustained rate without much AI assistance) is to exclude cases where the pace of capabilities would have slowed down a lot without AI automation, but we manage to keep the old trends going with dramatic uplift from AI.

The precise condition is: “It is plausible that this doubling is significantly attributable to the automation of research and/or engineering (as opposed to other factors, such as increased headcount, compute, or general productivity), such that continuation of the trend in AI progress seems likely to lead to even greater acceleration.”

The definition of the threshold is a bit finicky, partly because two speedup conditions must be met (speedup relative to the counterfactual and speedup relative to the past). But on a plain reading, my best guess is that it has been met. The authors of the METR report estimated roughly a doubling in the rate of progress relative to the past, but they only attribute roughly 80% of the current speed-up to AI, which would mean the central estimate for observed speedup relative to no AI was roughly ~1.8x as of the time of writing their report, in October 2026. But they also found that the contribution of AI has been growing over time, and I would guess that now (as of November 2026) it is appreciably higher. Even if the rate of progress is not fully double the counterfactual right this second, the threshold incorporates reasonable expectations (“We observe or expect double the rate of progress…”).

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei explains the idea of compute multipliers in this essay: “The field is constantly coming up with ideas, large and small, that make things more effective or efficient: it could be an improvement to the architecture of the model (a tweak to the basic Transformer architecture that all of today’s models use) or simply a way of running the model more efficiently on the underlying hardware. New generations of hardware also have the same effect. What this typically does is shift the curve: if the innovation is a 2x “compute multiplier” (CM), then it allows you to get 40% on a coding task for $5M instead of $10M; or 60% for $50M instead of $100M, etc. Every frontier AI company regularly discovers many of these CM’s: frequently small ones (~1.2x), sometimes medium-sized ones (~2x), and every once in a while very large ones (~10x).”

This is a simplified version of the modeling that my colleague Ryan Greenblatt goes through here. His numbers are based on his prior public modeling of the factors like the importance of software vs training compute and the importance of labor vs experimental compute.

Common formulations of Cobb-Douglas often measure labor as the number of humans. In that formulation, there needs to be a parallelization penalty on top of labor and the coefficients don’t add to 1. As I discuss below, I’m choosing to represent labor in terms of serial labor acceleration rather than the number of humans; this formulation was suggested by Ryan Greenblatt in this post.

To make the math simple, I just assumed that labor and experiment compute contributed equally (i.e. compute multipliers = (labor)^0.5 * (compute)^0.5. This means you square the acceleration in compute multipliers to back out the acceleration in labor. The range I gave omits uncertainty on the size of the contributions: for example if labor contributed ⅔, you would instead need to raise it to the power of 3/2.

I say several months rather than a full year because the report authors say that the initial faster progress in late 2025 was in significant part due to other factors, though the current speedup is mainly due to AI.

In practice, the 10x uplift is unlikely to be uniform: some tasks (e.g. more “hill-climbable” tasks) will be sped up much more than others, for a net productivity improvement as big as a 10x uniform acceleration.

That is, any number of people that actually exist. I’d guess it would be sufficient if you could clone all the existing researchers several hundred times, but there aren’t that many strong AI researchers and engineers in the world.

From the report: “A key concern is that acceleration from the use of AI could start to compound, as the development of more capable models leads to further acceleration. As we describe below, we did not identify any clear bottlenecks (limited inputs that would prevent further growth) or steep diminishing returns to further scaling that would get in the way of such compounding acceleration, though our understanding evolved several times over the course of this assessment, and we have significant uncertainty. Because of this, we believe there is a significant risk that further capabilities progress could lead to even faster acceleration over the coming year.”

This heuristic argument is consistent with simple but plausible models where recursive self-improvement makes the rate of AI progress exponential in cumulative AI progress (e.g., log effective compute); here is a derivation of this heuristic argument for one parametrization of this class of models.

AI R&D-5 was defined as: “The ability to cause dramatic acceleration in the rate of effective scaling. Specifically, this would be the case if we observed or projected an increase in the effective training compute of the world’s most capable model that, over the course of a year, was equivalent to two years of the average rate of progress during the period of early 2018 to early 2024. We roughly estimate that the 2018-2024 average scaleup was around 35x per year, so this would imply an actual or projected one-year scaleup of 35^2 = ~1000x.” It is weaker because it compares to the fixed 2018-2024 period rather than the fastest observed pace without significant AI acceleration, and it doesn’t technically require that you do any attribution to AI to meet the threshold.

The minimum commitment was to reach RAND’s ASL-4 security level; my understanding is that this is a level of security that would protect model weights from a routine operation by top state actors (e.g., China), but not from a top-priority operation. This is how Anthropic describes it: “At minimum, the ASL-4 Security Standard (which would protect against model-weight theft by state-level adversaries) is required, although we expect a higher security standard may be required.”

Anthropic is not the good guys, the Pacing letter was written to placate society long enough to get to RSI. They are trying to manifest this atrocity before the government can figure out how to stop them.
